and Row and its prodigy, Factor 1 requires consideration 
of three subfactors:  commerciality, bad faith, and 
transformativeness.  
The degree of transformativeness, if any, 
needs to be weighed against commerciality and bad faith.  
Here all three subfactors weigh against fair use.  
On commerciality there is simply no 
dispute that this use was commercial.  This Court has 
already found in Docket 547 that ROSS's uses were 
undoubtedly commercial, and one of its goals was to 
compete with Westlaw.
THE COURT:  Then ROSS didn't dispute 
commerciality.  
MS. CENDALI:  Absolutely not.  But this 
is important, Your Honor, though, under Warhol at 530 
and 31, because the Court there re-emphasizes the 
importance of commerciality, stating that when 
commentary has no critical bearing on the substance or 
style of the original, the claim and true fairness in 
borrowing from another's work diminishes accordingly.  
If it does not vanish and other factors, 
like the extent of commerciality, loom larger.  And 
that's this case.  
As to bad faith, ROSS' undisputed contact 
constitutes bad faith.  The Supreme Court held in

Harper & Row the propriety of the conduct as part of 
Factor One is fair use presupposes good faith and fair 
dealing.  
In Harper, the Supreme Court found the 
defendant knowingly exploited manuscript and ignored the 
other side's rights for its own benefits. 
This case is like Harper.  This isn't a 
case where the defendant merely reached out for a 
license and was denied; rather, ROSS expressly was told 
that accessing Westlaw for competitive purposes violated 
Westlaw's terms of service, yet it was undeterred.  
And, in fact, this case has a very 
unusual record filled with deceit in which ROSS in 
general here -- not naming names -- tried to get access 
to Westlaw by having its executives pretend to be law 
students, by convincing its law firm to share 
credentials, by having an employee get access pretending 
to be a solo practitioner; and, of course, by hiring 
LegalEase to do indirectly what ROSS could not do 
directly.  
The law, we submit, should not condone or 
encourage such behavior as the Second Circuit stated in 
Rogers v. Koons, copying in bad faith primarily for 
profit-making motives is not fair use.  This Court 
should find, respectfully, the bad faith subfactor based

against fair use.  
And to the final subfactor, 
transformativeness, ROSS cannot meet its burden of 
showing its use was transformative, let alone 
transformative enough to offset the significant 
commerciality and bad faith here.  
ROSS used the Westlaw content for the 
same purpose as plaintiffs, which under Warhol weighs 
against fair use.  As Warhol said at 531 and 532, the 
more similar the parties' purposes, the less 
transformative the use, because they're more likely to 
be substituted.  
Here the purposes are identical.  First, 
ROSS' overall purpose in copying the Westlaw content for 
the bulk memos was to create an admitted substitute, a 
replacement for Westlaw.  It's hard to find a more 
clear-cut case of substitution than the facts of this 
case.  
ROSS was not just doing an artwork that 
might have had some remote impact on someone else.  It 
was creating a product specifically designed to be a 
commercial substitute for Westlaw, and it was marketed 
that way to take market share from Westlaw, to bring 
about the death of Westlaw contract, marketing ads that 
said "ROSS or Westlaw"?

THE COURT:  Okay.  Now, your framework 
for arguing this is relying on the Second Circuit's 
decision in Authors Guild vs. Google.  Three factors:  
commerciality, bad faith, transformativeness.  
But I want to understand how I should 
read that after the Supreme Court's -- that's 2015 -- 
how should I read that after the Supreme Court's 2021 
decision in Google vs. Oracle.  They didn't decide good 
faith, yes or no, but there was some skepticism about 
whether it plays a role.  So how should I feel about 
relying on the bad faith consideration and how much, if 
any, weight should I give it post-Google?  
MS. CENDALI:  Well, two answers.  One, as 
Your Honor noted, the Court, in Google, just said, I'm 
skeptical about this, but the Court did not take the 
opportunity to overrule or change Harper, which remains 
the law of the land and the law that the district courts 
and the courts of appeals throughout the country have 
followed.  
Moreover, when you look closely, as I'm 
sure Your Honor has, that opinion, the Court cited, in 
particular, Campbell on that.  And the concerning 
Campbell there was about, Gee, I don't want to 
discourage people from reaching out and asking for a

769, is after Feist.  And what the Ninth Circuit held is 
that even after Feist, when you talk about nature of the 
work, you can look at more than just creativity, that 
you're not restricted.  Because the point there is, what 
is it -- you know, what do we want from the copyright 
system?  And maybe there is a work that, from the 
copyrightability point of view, has less creativity to 
it, but if it was really labor intensive to create, as a 
system we still want to have that protection.  
So Wall Data is one case on that subject.  
It cites to others that are pre-Feist, MCA versus 
Wilson.  And what's interesting about MCA v. Wilson is 
that case is favorably cited by the Supreme Court in 
Google v. Oracle.  So continuing vitality of that 
concept -- 
THE COURT:  Right.  But there is no 
dispute that sweat of the brow alone is not enough.

Question 5:  The circumstances under 
which it is appropriate for the judge to resolve 
substantial similarity at summary judgement rather than 
leave it to a jury, and I wanted specific citations to 
case law.  
MR. SIMMONS:  Your Honor, courts 
routinely find that two works are substantially similar 
without requiring a jury.  Just the level set.  As with 
other issues that are presented for summary judgement, 
the question is whether there are genuine disputes as to 
material facts.  When the facts are not disputed, just 
the legal interpretations of them, summary judgement is 
appropriate.  
So for example, in TD Bank vs. Hill, the

Third Circuit held that the district court correctly 
granted summary judgement on copyright infringement. 
That's 928 F.3d 259, 277.  Neither the Third Circuit nor 
the district court that affirmed applied special 
standard.  Instead, the courts considered the 
defendant's arguments that --
(Court reporter interruption.)
MR. SIMMONS:  I'm sorry. 
The Court considered the defendant's 
arguments that the material they copied was 
unprotectable under the merger or set of fair doctrines.  
And because there were numerous ways to express the life 
story and business philosophy at issue, they did not 
apply.  Thus, the copied material was protectable and 
summary judgement was appropriate.  That's at 278.  
The same thing happened in a case Ms. 
Cendali and I had in the District of New Jersey.  That 
is in Tetris versus Xio Interactive.  The Court 
considered the defendant's arguments for holding that 
the copy material from Tetris, the video game, was 
unprotectable under the idea, merger, Santa Fer, game 
mechanics doctrines.  It filtered out the unprotectable 
material and then compared what remained.  And as they 
were substantially similar, the Court granted summary 
judgment.  That's 863 F. Supp. 2d 394, starting at 408.

tells you at Section 12.10 that if the resemblance is 
overwhelming, you should grant summary judgement.  In 
footnote 76, notes that it rarely happens in favor of 
plaintiff and usually on substantial similarity it's 
been in favor of the defendant.  
And in each of the cases that were cited, 
the correlation was obvious.  So TD Bank copied 16 
percent, side-by-side comparison in Unicolors.  So 
overwhelmingly identical fabric designs.  Range Rd. 
Music, the entire song was played.  It wasn't a matter 
of parsing anything.  Association of American Medical, a 
side-by-side comparison was done by the court.  The 
original aspects of the MCAT had been improperly 
appropriated.  Side-by-side comparison, the court said 
there was no difference.  So we are not at that stage.  
We are at a stage, though, where -- 
again, I will say it again, we would like to respond to 
the Krein supplemental.  But moreover, this case also 
raises the issue of copying, and that's the example we 
talked about last time.

judgment on the claims.
What the law of the case doctrine does is 
the Supreme Court in Christianson versus Colt Industries 
explained, is provide a rule of practice for when a 
Court exercises its discretion that would promote 
finality and efficiency.  That's 486 U.S. 800 at 8916.
In re: Pharmacy Benefit Managers 
antitrust litigation, the Third Circuit emphasized the 
importance of maintaining consistency and avoiding 
reconsideration of matters once decided during the 
course of a single continuing lawsuit.  That's 582 F3d 
432 at 439.  
Now, this applies to both conclusions of 
law and findings of factual findings as noted in 
Kagerise versus Susquehanna Township School District.  
That's 325 F.supp 3d 564 at 589.  
Thus, for example, this Court previously 
concluded that loss actual copying at a prior opinion  
and that copyrighting the com piliation extends to the 
copy writable pieces of that compilation.  That's the 
opinion at 5.
Given that the parties proceeded to trial 
and briefed summary judgement, with those holdings in 
mind it would be the epiphysis of finality to reconsider 
those now.

That being said, it is not to say the 
Court can't reconsider things in its prior ruling.  The 
Third Circuit in Pharmacy Benefit articulated when rules 
could be reconsidered under that rule of practice.
The two most clear examples are Ruten 
Kristen which afforded them as extraordinary 
circumstances.  Those are decisions clearly erroneous 
from where they would manifest injustice.  That's at 
816.
But the Third Circuit also recognizes 
when new evidence is available or a law has been 
announced.  That's Pharmacy Benefit at 582 F3d at 439.
And, finally, the trial judge, and I'm 
quoting here, is not precluded from clarifying or 
correcting an earlier ambiguous ruling or reconsidering 
an issue when it appears a previous ruling even if 
unambiguous might lead to an unjust result.  That's 
again Pharmacy Benefit 439.
So here the Court shouldn't change the 
issues that were conclusively decided but where the 
Court left things open or was otherwise thinking that it 
would be a better course to change them, you still have 
the power to reconsider.  It's just a question of how do 
you exercise your discretion to do so.
